Not to be confused with <a href="/wiki/Thomas_%C3%A0_Beckett_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="Thomas à Beckett (disambiguation)">Thomas à Beckett (disambiguation)</a>.</span> <span>For the school in Northampton, see <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Becket_Catholic_School" title="Thomas Becket Catholic School">Thomas Becket Catholic School</a>. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Beckett_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="Thomas Beckett (disambiguation)">Thomas Beckett</a>.</span></div>

href="/wiki/Exeter_College,_Oxford" title="Exeter College, Oxford">Exeter College, Oxford</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/Portsmouth" title="Portsmouth">Portsmouth</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/Arbroath_Abbey" title="Arbroath Abbey">Arbroath Abbey</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/Secular_clergy" title="Secular clergy">secular clergy</a></li><li><a href="/wiki/City_of_London" title="City of London">City of London</a></li></ul></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Shrines</th><td class="infobox-data">Canterbury Cathedral</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Cult suppressed</th><td class="infobox-data">1538 (by <a href="/wiki/Henry_VIII" title="Henry VIII">Henry VIII</a>)</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:#eee;line-height:normal;padding:0.2em;"><a href="/wiki/Lord_Chancellor" title="Lord Chancellor">Lord Chancellor</a></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data" style="border-bottom:none"><span class="nowrap"><b>In office</b></span><br />1155–1162</td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left">Monarch</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Henry_II_of_England" title="Henry II of England">Henry II</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><span class="nowrap">Preceded by</span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Robert_of_Ghent" title="Robert of Ghent">Robert of Ghent</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><span class="nowrap">Succeeded by</span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_Ridel_(bishop_of_Ely)" title="Geoffrey Ridel (bishop of Ely)">Geoffrey Ridel</a></td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">
</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">
</td></tr></tbody></table>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:BecketArms.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/BecketArms.svg/220px-BecketArms.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="253" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/BecketArms.svg/330px-BecketArms.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/BecketArms.svg/440px-BecketArms.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="578" data-file-height="666" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Attributed_arms" title="Attributed arms">Attributed arms</a> of Saint Thomas Becket: <i>Argent, three <a href="/wiki/Cornwall" title="Cornwall">Cornish</a> <a href="/wiki/Red-billed_chough" title="Red-billed chough">choughs</a> proper</i>, visible in many English churches dedicated to him. As he died 30 to 45 years before the age of heraldry, he bore no arms.</figcaption></figure>
<p><b>Thomas Becket</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;b&#39; in &#39;buy&#39;">b</span><span title="/ɛ/: &#39;e&#39; in &#39;dress&#39;">ɛ</span><span title="&#39;k&#39; in &#39;kind&#39;">k</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="&#39;t&#39; in &#39;tie&#39;">t</span></span>/</a></span></span>), also known as <b>Saint Thomas of Canterbury</b>, <b>Thomas of London</b><sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> and later <b>Thomas à Becket</b><sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;note 1&#93;</a></sup> (21 December 1119 or 1120&#160;– 29 December 1170), served as <a href="/wiki/Lord_Chancellor" title="Lord Chancellor">Lord Chancellor</a> from 1155 to 1162, and then notably as <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Canterbury" title="Archbishop of Canterbury">Archbishop of Canterbury</a> from 1162 until his death in 1170. He engaged in conflict with <a href="/wiki/Henry_II_of_England" title="Henry II of England">Henry II, King of England</a>, over the rights and privileges of the Church and was murdered by followers of the King in <a href="/wiki/Canterbury_Cathedral" title="Canterbury Cathedral">Canterbury Cathedral</a>. Soon after his death, he was <a href="/wiki/Canonization" title="Canonization">canonised</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_III" title="Pope Alexander III">Pope Alexander III</a>.  He is venerated as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> and <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyr</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Sources">Sources</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Becket&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Sources">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The main sources for the life of Becket are a number of biographies written by contemporaries. A few of these documents are by unknown writers, although traditional historiography has given them names. The known biographers are <a href="/wiki/John_of_Salisbury" title="John of Salisbury">John of Salisbury</a>, <a href="/wiki/Edward_Grim" title="Edward Grim">Edward Grim</a>, <a href="/wiki/Benedict_of_Peterborough" title="Benedict of Peterborough">Benedict of Peterborough</a>, <a href="/wiki/William_of_Canterbury" title="William of Canterbury">William of Canterbury</a>, <a href="/wiki/William_fitzStephen" class="mw-redirect" title="William fitzStephen">William fitzStephen</a>, <a href="/wiki/Guernes_de_Pont-Sainte-Maxence" title="Guernes de Pont-Sainte-Maxence">Guernes of Pont-Sainte-Maxence</a>, <a href="/wiki/Robert_of_Cricklade" title="Robert of Cricklade">Robert of Cricklade</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alan_of_Tewkesbury" title="Alan of Tewkesbury">Alan of Tewkesbury</a>, <a href="/wiki/Benet_of_St_Albans" title="Benet of St Albans">Benet of St Albans</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Herbert_of_Bosham" title="Herbert of Bosham">Herbert of Bosham</a>. The other biographers, who remain anonymous, are generally given the pseudonyms of Anonymous I, Anonymous II (or Anonymous of Lambeth), and Anonymous III (or Lansdowne Anonymous). Besides these accounts, there are also two other accounts that are likely contemporary that appear in the <i>Quadrilogus II</i> and the <span title="Icelandic-language text"><i lang="is"><a href="/wiki/Th%C3%B3mas_saga_Erkibyskups" title="Thómas saga Erkibyskups">Thómas saga Erkibyskups</a></i></span>. Besides these biographies, there is also the mention of the events of Becket's life in the chroniclers of the time. These include <a href="/wiki/Robert_of_Torigni" title="Robert of Torigni">Robert of Torigni</a>'s work, <a href="/wiki/Roger_of_Howden" title="Roger of Howden">Roger of Howden</a>'s <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Gesta Regis Henrici Secundi</i></span> and <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Chronica</i></span>, <a href="/wiki/Ralph_Diceto" class="mw-redirect" title="Ralph Diceto">Ralph Diceto</a>'s works, <a href="/wiki/William_of_Newburgh" title="William of Newburgh">William of Newburgh</a>'s <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Historia Rerum</i></span>, and <a href="/wiki/Gervase_of_Canterbury" title="Gervase of Canterbury">Gervase of Canterbury</a>'s works.<sup id="cite_ref-Barlow3_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barlow3-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Becket&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Becket was born c. 1119,<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> or in 1120 according to later tradition,<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> at <a href="/wiki/Cheapside" title="Cheapside">Cheapside</a>, London, on 21 December, the feast day of St Thomas the Apostle. He was the son of Gilbert and Matilda Beket.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;note 2&#93;</a></sup> Gilbert's father was from <a href="/wiki/Thierville" title="Thierville">Thierville</a> in the lordship of Brionne in <a href="/wiki/Normandy" title="Normandy">Normandy</a>, and was either a small landowner or a petty knight.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Matilda was also of Norman descent<sup id="cite_ref-Barlow11_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barlow11-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> – her family may have originated near <a href="/wiki/Caen" title="Caen">Caen</a>. Gilbert was perhaps related to <a href="/wiki/Theobald_of_Bec" title="Theobald of Bec">Theobald of Bec</a>, whose family was also from Thierville. Gilbert began his life as a merchant, perhaps in textiles, but by the 1120s he was living in London and was a property owner, living on the rental income from his properties. He also served as the sheriff of the city at some point.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Becket's parents were buried in <a href="/wiki/Old_St_Paul%27s_Cathedral" title="Old St Paul&#39;s Cathedral">Old St Paul's Cathedral</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Thomas_Becket_Memorial_Plaque_on_Cheapside.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Thomas_Becket_Memorial_Plaque_on_Cheapside.jpg/220px-Thomas_Becket_Memorial_Plaque_on_Cheapside.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Thomas_Becket_Memorial_Plaque_on_Cheapside.jpg/330px-Thomas_Becket_Memorial_Plaque_on_Cheapside.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Thomas_Becket_Memorial_Plaque_on_Cheapside.jpg/440px-Thomas_Becket_Memorial_Plaque_on_Cheapside.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4639" data-file-height="3092" /></a><figcaption>Plaque marking Becket's birthplace along Cheapside</figcaption></figure>
<p>One of Becket's father's wealthy friends, Richer de <a href="/wiki/L%27Aigle" title="L&#39;Aigle">L'Aigle</a>, often invited Thomas to his estates in <a href="/wiki/Sussex" title="Sussex">Sussex</a>, where Becket encountered hunting and hawking. According to Grim, Becket learned much from Richer, who was later a signatory of the <a href="/wiki/Constitutions_of_Clarendon" title="Constitutions of Clarendon">Constitutions of Clarendon</a> against him.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the age of 10, Becket was sent as a student to <a href="/wiki/Merton_Priory" title="Merton Priory">Merton Priory</a> south-west of the city in <a href="/wiki/Surrey" title="Surrey">Surrey</a>. He later attended a grammar school in London, perhaps the one at St Paul's Cathedral. He did not study any subjects beyond the <a href="/wiki/Trivium_(education)" class="mw-redirect" title="Trivium (education)">trivium</a> and <a href="/wiki/Quadrivium" title="Quadrivium">quadrivium</a> at these schools. Around the age of 20, he spent about a year in Paris, but he did not study canon or civil law at the time and his <a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a> skill always remained somewhat rudimentary. Some time after Becket began his schooling, Gilbert Becket suffered financial reverses and the younger Becket was forced to earn a living as a clerk. Gilbert first secured a place for his son in the business of a relative – Osbert Huitdeniers. Later Becket acquired a position in the household of Theobald of Bec, by then <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Canterbury" title="Archbishop of Canterbury">Archbishop of Canterbury</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Theobald entrusted him with several important missions to Rome and also sent him to <a href="/wiki/Bologna" title="Bologna">Bologna</a> and <a href="/wiki/Auxerre" title="Auxerre">Auxerre</a> to study <a href="/wiki/Canon_law" title="Canon law">canon law</a>. In 1154, Theobald named Becket <a href="/wiki/List_of_Archdeacons_of_Canterbury" class="mw-redirect" title="List of Archdeacons of Canterbury">Archdeacon of Canterbury</a>, and other ecclesiastical offices included a number of <a href="/wiki/Benefice" title="Benefice">benefices</a>, <a href="/wiki/Prebendary" title="Prebendary">prebends</a> at <a href="/wiki/Lincoln_Cathedral" title="Lincoln Cathedral">Lincoln Cathedral</a> and St Paul's Cathedral, and the office of <a href="/wiki/Provost_(religion)" title="Provost (religion)">Provost</a> of <a href="/wiki/Beverley" title="Beverley">Beverley</a>. His efficiency in those posts led Theobald to recommend him to King <a href="/wiki/Henry_II_of_England" title="Henry II of England">Henry II</a> for the vacant post of <a href="/wiki/Lord_Chancellor" title="Lord Chancellor">Lord Chancellor</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> to which Becket was appointed in January 1155.<sup id="cite_ref-Handbook84_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Handbook84-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Chancellor, Becket enforced the king's traditional sources of revenue that were exacted from all landowners, including churches and bishoprics.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> King Henry sent his son <a href="/wiki/Henry_the_Young_King" title="Henry the Young King">Henry</a> to live in Becket's household, it being the custom then for noble children to be fostered out to other noble houses.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (March 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Primacy">Primacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Becket&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Primacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Becket was nominated as Archbishop of Canterbury in 1162, several months after the death of Theobald. His election was confirmed on 23 May 1162 by a royal council of bishops and noblemen.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Henry may have hoped that Becket would continue to put royal government first, rather than the church, but the famed transformation of Becket into an <a href="/wiki/Ascetic" class="mw-redirect" title="Ascetic">ascetic</a> occurred at this time.<sup id="cite_ref-Huscroft192_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Huscroft192-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-packed center">
	<li class="gallerycaption">Becket enthroned as Archbishop of Canterbury from a <a href="/wiki/Nottingham_Alabaster" class="mw-redirect" title="Nottingham Alabaster">Nottingham Alabaster</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Victoria_%26_Albert_Museum" class="mw-redirect" title="Victoria &amp; Albert Museum">Victoria &amp; Albert Museum</a></li>
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 202px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 200px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:StThomasEnthroned.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/StThomasEnthroned.jpg/300px-StThomasEnthroned.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="200" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/StThomasEnthroned.jpg/450px-StThomasEnthroned.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/StThomasEnthroned.jpg/600px-StThomasEnthroned.jpg 2x" data-file-width="768" data-file-height="768" /></a></span></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">
			</div>
		</li>
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 202px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 200px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:StThomasReturn.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/12/StThomasReturn.jpg/300px-StThomasReturn.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="200" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/12/StThomasReturn.jpg/450px-StThomasReturn.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/12/StThomasReturn.jpg/600px-StThomasReturn.jpg 2x" data-file-width="768" data-file-height="768" /></a></span></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">
			</div>
		</li>
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 202px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 200px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:StThomasSens.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/StThomasSens.jpg/300px-StThomasSens.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="200" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/StThomasSens.jpg/450px-StThomasSens.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/StThomasSens.jpg/600px-StThomasSens.jpg 2x" data-file-width="768" data-file-height="768" /></a></span></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">
			</div>
		</li>
</ul>
<p>Becket was ordained a priest on 2 June 1162 at Canterbury, and on 3 June 1162 was <a href="/wiki/Consecration#Ordination_of_bishops" class="mw-redirect" title="Consecration">consecrated</a> as archbishop by <a href="/wiki/Henry_of_Blois" title="Henry of Blois">Henry of Blois</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Winchester" title="Bishop of Winchester">Bishop of Winchester</a> and the other <a href="/wiki/Suffragan_bishop" title="Suffragan bishop">suffragan bishops</a> of Canterbury.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A rift grew between Henry and Becket as the new archbishop resigned his <a href="/wiki/Chancellor" title="Chancellor">chancellorship</a> and sought to recover and extend the rights of the <a href="/wiki/Archbishopric" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishopric">archbishopric</a>. This led to a series of conflicts with the King, including one over the jurisdiction of secular courts over English clergymen, which accelerated antipathy between Becket and the king. Attempts by Henry to influence other bishops against Becket began in <a href="/wiki/Westminster_Abbey" title="Westminster Abbey">Westminster</a> in October 1163, where the King sought approval of the traditional rights of royal government in regard to the church.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> This led to the <a href="/wiki/Constitutions_of_Clarendon" title="Constitutions of Clarendon">Constitutions of Clarendon</a>, where Becket was officially asked to agree to the King's rights or face political repercussions.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Constitutions_of_Clarendon">Constitutions of Clarendon</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Becket&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Constitutions of Clarendon">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Becket_controversy" title="Becket controversy">Becket controversy</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Constitutions_of_Clarendon" title="Constitutions of Clarendon">Constitutions of Clarendon</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jindrich2_Beckett.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Manuscript illustration. The central man is wearing robes and a mitre and is facing the seated figure on the left. The seated man is wearing a crown and robes and is gesturing at the mitred man. Behind the mitred figure are a number of standing men wearing armour and carrying weapons." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/Jindrich2_Beckett.jpg/220px-Jindrich2_Beckett.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="187" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/Jindrich2_Beckett.jpg/330px-Jindrich2_Beckett.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/Jindrich2_Beckett.jpg/440px-Jindrich2_Beckett.jpg 2x" data-file-width="470" data-file-height="400" /></a><figcaption>14th-century depiction of Becket with King Henry II</figcaption></figure>
<p>King Henry II presided over assemblies of most of the higher English clergy at <a href="/wiki/Clarendon_Palace" title="Clarendon Palace">Clarendon Palace</a> on 30 January 1164. In 16 constitutions he sought less clerical independence and weaker connections with Rome. He used his skills to induce their consent and apparently succeeded with all but Becket. Finally, even Becket expressed willingness to agree to the substance of the <a href="/wiki/Constitutions_of_Clarendon" title="Constitutions of Clarendon">Constitutions of Clarendon</a>, but he still refused formally to sign the documents. Henry summoned Becket to appear before a great council at <a href="/wiki/Northampton_Castle" title="Northampton Castle">Northampton Castle</a> on 8 October 1164, to answer allegations of contempt of royal authority and <a href="/wiki/Malfeasance" class="mw-redirect" title="Malfeasance">malfeasance</a> in the Chancellor's office. Convicted on the charges, Becket stormed out of the trial and fled to the <a href="/wiki/Continental_Europe" title="Continental Europe">Continent</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Henry pursued the fugitive archbishop with a series of <a href="/wiki/Edict" title="Edict">edicts</a>, targeting Becket and all Becket's friends and supporters, but King <a href="/wiki/Louis_VII_of_France" title="Louis VII of France">Louis VII of France</a> offered Becket protection. He spent nearly two years in the <a href="/wiki/Cistercian" class="mw-redirect" title="Cistercian">Cistercian</a> <a href="/wiki/Abbey" title="Abbey">abbey</a> of <a href="/wiki/Pontigny_Abbey" title="Pontigny Abbey">Pontigny</a>, until Henry's threats against the order obliged him to return to <a href="/wiki/Sens" title="Sens">Sens</a>. Becket fought back by threatening <a href="/wiki/Excommunication" title="Excommunication">excommunication</a> and an <a href="/wiki/Interdict" title="Interdict">interdict</a> against the king and bishops and the kingdom, but <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_III" title="Pope Alexander III">Pope Alexander III</a>, though sympathising with him in theory, favoured a more diplomatic approach. <a href="/wiki/Papal_legates" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal legates">Papal legates</a> were sent in 1167 with authority to act as arbitrators.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Arbroath_Abbey_Seal_01.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Arbroath_Abbey_Seal_01.jpg/220px-Arbroath_Abbey_Seal_01.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="182" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Arbroath_Abbey_Seal_01.jpg/330px-Arbroath_Abbey_Seal_01.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Arbroath_Abbey_Seal_01.jpg/440px-Arbroath_Abbey_Seal_01.jpg 2x" data-file-width="744" data-file-height="616" /></a><figcaption>A <a href="/wiki/Seal_(device)" class="mw-redirect" title="Seal (device)">Seal</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Abbot_of_Arbroath" title="Abbot of Arbroath">Abbot of Arbroath</a>, showing the murder of Becket. <a href="/wiki/Arbroath_Abbey" title="Arbroath Abbey">Arbroath Abbey</a> was founded 8 years after the death of St Thomas and dedicated to him; it became the wealthiest abbey in Scotland.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1170, Alexander sent delegates to impose a solution to the dispute. At that point, Henry offered a compromise that would allow Thomas to return to England from exile.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Assassination">Assassination</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Becket&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Assassination">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Reliquary_Thomas_Becket_MNMA_Cl23296.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Reliquary_Thomas_Becket_MNMA_Cl23296.jpg/220px-Reliquary_Thomas_Becket_MNMA_Cl23296.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Reliquary_Thomas_Becket_MNMA_Cl23296.jpg/330px-Reliquary_Thomas_Becket_MNMA_Cl23296.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Reliquary_Thomas_Becket_MNMA_Cl23296.jpg/440px-Reliquary_Thomas_Becket_MNMA_Cl23296.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="2000" /></a><figcaption>Becket's assassination and funeral, from a French enamelled <a href="/wiki/Chasse_(casket)" title="Chasse (casket)">chasse</a> made about 1190–1200, one of about 52 surviving examples.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Place_of_murder_in_Canterbury_cathedral.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Place_of_murder_in_Canterbury_cathedral.jpg/170px-Place_of_murder_in_Canterbury_cathedral.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Place_of_murder_in_Canterbury_cathedral.jpg/255px-Place_of_murder_in_Canterbury_cathedral.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Place_of_murder_in_Canterbury_cathedral.jpg/340px-Place_of_murder_in_Canterbury_cathedral.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2448" data-file-height="3264" /></a><figcaption>Sculpture and altar marking the spot of Thomas Becket's martyrdom, Canterbury Cathedral. The sculpture by Giles Blomfield represents the knights' four swords (two metal swords with reddened tips and their two shadows).</figcaption></figure>
<p>In June 1170, <a href="/wiki/Roger_de_Pont_L%27%C3%89v%C3%AAque" title="Roger de Pont L&#39;Évêque">Roger de Pont L'Évêque</a>, Archbishop of York, was at York with <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_Foliot" title="Gilbert Foliot">Gilbert Foliot</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_London" title="Bishop of London">Bishop of London</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Josceline_de_Bohon" title="Josceline de Bohon">Josceline de Bohon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Salisbury" title="Bishop of Salisbury">Bishop of Salisbury</a>, to <a href="/wiki/Coronation#Coronation_of_heirs_apparent" title="Coronation">crown</a> the heir apparent, <a href="/wiki/Henry_the_Young_King" title="Henry the Young King">Henry the Young King</a>. This breached Canterbury's privilege of coronation and in November 1170 Becket excommunicated all three.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On hearing reports of Becket's actions, Henry is said to have uttered words interpreted by his men as wishing Becket killed.<sup id="cite_ref-Huscroft194_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Huscroft194-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> The exact wording is in doubt and several versions were reported.<sup id="cite_ref-Warren508_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Warren508-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The most commonly quoted, as invented in 1740 and handed down by oral tradition, is "<a href="/wiki/Will_no_one_rid_me_of_this_turbulent_priest%3F" title="Will no one rid me of this turbulent priest?">Will no one rid me of this turbulent priest?</a>",<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> but according to historian <a href="/wiki/Simon_Schama" title="Simon Schama">Simon Schama</a> this is incorrect: he accepts the account of the contemporary biographer Edward Grim, writing in Latin, who gives, "What miserable drones and traitors have I nourished and brought up in my household, who let their lord be treated with such shameful contempt by a low-born cleric?"<sup id="cite_ref-Schama1_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Schama1-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Many other variants have found their way into popular culture.
</p><p>Regardless of what Henry said, it was interpreted as a royal command. Four knights,<sup id="cite_ref-Huscroft194_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Huscroft194-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Reginald_FitzUrse" class="mw-redirect" title="Reginald FitzUrse">Reginald FitzUrse</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hugh_de_Morville,_Lord_of_Westmorland" title="Hugh de Morville, Lord of Westmorland">Hugh de Morville</a>, <a href="/wiki/William_de_Tracy" title="William de Tracy">William de Tracy</a> and <a href="/wiki/Richard_le_Breton" title="Richard le Breton">Richard le Breton</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> set out to confront the Archbishop of Canterbury. On 29 December 1170, they arrived at Canterbury. According to accounts by the monk Gervase of Canterbury and eyewitness Edward Grim, the knights placed their weapons under a tree outside the cathedral and hid their armour under cloaks before entering to challenge Becket. The knights told Becket he was to go to <a href="/wiki/Winchester" title="Winchester">Winchester</a> to give an account of his actions, but Becket refused. Not until he refused their demands to submit to the king's will did they retrieve their weapons and rush back inside for the killing.<sup id="cite_ref-APS_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-APS-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Becket, meanwhile, proceeded to the main hall for <a href="/wiki/Vespers" title="Vespers">vespers</a>. The other monks tried to bolt themselves in for safety, but Becket said to them, "It is not right to make a fortress out of the house of prayer!", ordering them to reopen the doors.
</p><p>The four knights, wielding drawn swords, ran into the room crying, "Where is Thomas Becket, traitor to the King and country?" They found Becket in a spot near a door to the monastic cloister, the stairs into the crypt, and the stairs leading up into the <a href="/wiki/Choir_(architecture)" title="Choir (architecture)">quire</a> of the cathedral, where the monks were chanting vespers.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> On seeing them, Becket said, "I am no traitor and I am ready to die." One knight grabbed him and tried to pull him outside, but Becket grabbed onto a pillar and bowed his head to make peace with God.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Several contemporary accounts of what happened next exist; of particular note is that of Grim, who was wounded in the attack. This is part of his account:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>...the impious knight... suddenly set upon him and [shaved] off the summit of his crown which the sacred chrism consecrated to God... Then, with another blow received on the head, he remained firm. But with the third the stricken martyr bent his knees and elbows, offering himself as a living sacrifice, saying in a low voice, "For the name of Jesus and the protection of the church, I am ready to embrace death." But the third knight inflicted a grave wound on the fallen one; with this blow... his crown, which was large, separated from his head so that the blood turned white from the brain yet no less did the brain turn red from the blood; it purpled the appearance of the church... The fifth – not a knight but a cleric who had entered with the knights... placed his foot on the neck of the holy priest and precious martyr and (it is horrible to say) scattered the brains with the blood across the floor, exclaiming to the rest, "We can leave this place, knights, he will not get up again."<sup id="cite_ref-Lee2012_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lee2012-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Another account appears in <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Expugnatio Hibernica</i></span> ("Conquest of Ireland", 1189) by <a href="/wiki/Giraldus_Cambrensis" class="mw-redirect" title="Giraldus Cambrensis">Gerald of Wales</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ivory_carving_St._Thomas_a_Becket.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/Ivory_carving_St._Thomas_a_Becket.jpg/220px-Ivory_carving_St._Thomas_a_Becket.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/Ivory_carving_St._Thomas_a_Becket.jpg/330px-Ivory_carving_St._Thomas_a_Becket.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/Ivory_carving_St._Thomas_a_Becket.jpg/440px-Ivory_carving_St._Thomas_a_Becket.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3465" data-file-height="3465" /></a><figcaption>An ivory piece portraying the knights involved in Becket's assassination. One knight holds an axe with which to break down the door of the Cathedral.</figcaption></figure>
<h2><span id="After_Becket.27s_death"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="After_Becket's_death">After Becket's death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Becket&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: After Becket&#039;s death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>After his death, the monks prepared Becket's body for burial.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> According to some accounts, it was found that Becket had worn a <a href="/wiki/Cilice" title="Cilice">hairshirt</a> under his archbishop's garments – a sign of penance.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Soon after, the faithful throughout Europe began venerating Becket as a <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyr</a>, and on 21 February 1173 – little more than two years after his death – he was <a href="/wiki/Canonised" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonised">canonised</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Alexander_III" title="Pope Alexander III">Pope Alexander III</a> in St Peter's Church, <a href="/wiki/Segni" title="Segni">Segni</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> In 1173, Becket's sister Mary was appointed <a href="/wiki/Abbess" title="Abbess">Abbess</a> of <a href="/wiki/Barking_Abbey" title="Barking Abbey">Barking</a> as reparation for the murder of her brother.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> On 12 July 1174, amidst the <a href="/wiki/Revolt_of_1173%E2%80%9374" class="mw-redirect" title="Revolt of 1173–74">Revolt of 1173–74</a>, Henry humbled himself in public <a href="/wiki/Penance" title="Penance">penance</a> at Becket's tomb and at the church of <a href="/wiki/St._Dunstan%27s,_Canterbury" title="St. Dunstan&#39;s, Canterbury">St. Dunstan's</a>, which became a most popular <a href="/wiki/Christian_pilgrimage" title="Christian pilgrimage">pilgrimage</a> site.
</p><p>Becket's assassins fled north to de Morville's <a href="/wiki/Knaresborough_Castle" title="Knaresborough Castle">Knaresborough Castle</a> for about a year. De Morville also held property in <a href="/wiki/Cumbria" title="Cumbria">Cumbria</a> and this too may have provided a hiding place, as the men prepared for a longer stay in the separate kingdom of Scotland. They were not arrested and Henry did not confiscate their lands, but he did not help them when they sought his advice in August 1171. Pope Alexander excommunicated all four. Seeking forgiveness, the assassins travelled to Rome, where the Pope ordered them to serve as knights in the Holy Lands for a period of 14 years.<sup id="cite_ref-Barlow_pp._257_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barlow_pp._257-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This sentence also inspired the <a href="/wiki/Knights_of_Saint_Thomas" title="Knights of Saint Thomas">Knights of Saint Thomas</a>, incorporated in 1191 at <a href="/wiki/Acre,_Israel#Second_Crusader_Kingdom_of_Jerusalem_(1191-1291)" title="Acre, Israel">Acre</a>, and which was to be modelled on the <a href="/wiki/Teutonic_Knights" class="mw-redirect" title="Teutonic Knights">Teutonic Knights</a>. This was the only military order native to England (with chapters in not only Acre, but London, Kilkenny, and Nicosia), just as the <a href="/wiki/Gilbertine_Order" title="Gilbertine Order">Gilbertine Order</a> was the only monastic order native to England. <a href="/wiki/Henry_VIII" title="Henry VIII">Henry VIII</a> dissolved both of these during the Reformation, rather than merging them with foreign orders or nationalising them as elements of the Protestant Church of England.
</p><p>The monks were afraid Becket's body might be stolen, and so his remains were placed beneath the floor of the eastern <a href="/wiki/Crypt" title="Crypt">crypt</a> of the cathedral.<sup id="cite_ref-Barlow_pp._257_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barlow_pp._257-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> A stone cover over it had two holes where <a href="/wiki/Christian_pilgrimage" title="Christian pilgrimage">pilgrims</a> could insert their heads and kiss the tomb,<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> as illustrated in the "Miracle Windows" of the Trinity Chapel. A guard chamber (now the Wax Chamber) had a clear view of the grave. In 1220, Becket's bones were moved to a new gold-plated, bejewelled shrine behind the high altar in the Trinity Chapel.<sup id="cite_ref-angl_Beck_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-angl_Beck-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> The shrine was supported by three pairs of pillars on a raised platform with three steps. This is shown in one of the miracle windows. Canterbury's religious history had always brought many pilgrims, and after Becket's death the numbers rapidly rose further.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Cult_in_the_Middle_Ages">Cult in the Middle Ages</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Becket&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Cult in the Middle Ages">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:158_Santa_Maria_de_Terrassa,_cicle_de_Tom%C3%A0s_Becket.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/158_Santa_Maria_de_Terrassa%2C_cicle_de_Tom%C3%A0s_Becket.jpg/200px-158_Santa_Maria_de_Terrassa%2C_cicle_de_Tom%C3%A0s_Becket.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="267" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/158_Santa_Maria_de_Terrassa%2C_cicle_de_Tom%C3%A0s_Becket.jpg/300px-158_Santa_Maria_de_Terrassa%2C_cicle_de_Tom%C3%A0s_Becket.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2f/158_Santa_Maria_de_Terrassa%2C_cicle_de_Tom%C3%A0s_Becket.jpg/400px-158_Santa_Maria_de_Terrassa%2C_cicle_de_Tom%C3%A0s_Becket.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2592" data-file-height="3456" /></a><figcaption>St Thomas Becket's consecration, death and burial, at wall paintings in Santa Maria de Terrassa (<a href="/wiki/Terrassa" title="Terrassa">Terrassa</a>, Catalonia, Spain), romanesque frescoes, c. 1180<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Canterbury_-_Ehemaliger_Standort_des_Schreins_von_Thomas_Becket.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Canterbury_-_Ehemaliger_Standort_des_Schreins_von_Thomas_Becket.jpg/220px-Canterbury_-_Ehemaliger_Standort_des_Schreins_von_Thomas_Becket.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Canterbury_-_Ehemaliger_Standort_des_Schreins_von_Thomas_Becket.jpg/330px-Canterbury_-_Ehemaliger_Standort_des_Schreins_von_Thomas_Becket.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/50/Canterbury_-_Ehemaliger_Standort_des_Schreins_von_Thomas_Becket.jpg/440px-Canterbury_-_Ehemaliger_Standort_des_Schreins_von_Thomas_Becket.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2048" data-file-height="1536" /></a><figcaption>Candle marking the former spot of the shrine of Thomas Becket, at Canterbury Cathedral</figcaption></figure>
<p>In Scotland, King <a href="/wiki/William_the_Lion" title="William the Lion">William the Lion</a> ordered the building of <a href="/wiki/Arbroath_Abbey" title="Arbroath Abbey">Arbroath Abbey</a> in 1178. On completion in 1197 the new foundation was dedicated to Becket, whom the king had known personally while at the English court as a young man.
</p><p>On 7 July 1220, the 50th <a href="/wiki/Golden_jubilee" title="Golden jubilee">jubilee</a> year of his death, Becket's remains were moved from his first tomb to a <a href="/wiki/Shrine" title="Shrine">shrine</a> in the recently built Trinity Chapel.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> This <a href="/wiki/Translation_(relic)" title="Translation (relic)">translation</a> was "one of the great symbolic events in the life of the medieval English Church", attended by King <a href="/wiki/Henry_III_of_England" title="Henry III of England">Henry III</a>, the papal legate, the Archbishop of Canterbury <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Langton" title="Stephen Langton">Stephen Langton</a> and many dignitaries and magnates secular and ecclesiastical. 
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Martirio_di_Thomas_Becket_-_chiesa_di_San_Lanfranco.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Martirio_di_Thomas_Becket_-_chiesa_di_San_Lanfranco.jpg/200px-Martirio_di_Thomas_Becket_-_chiesa_di_San_Lanfranco.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="131" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Martirio_di_Thomas_Becket_-_chiesa_di_San_Lanfranco.jpg/300px-Martirio_di_Thomas_Becket_-_chiesa_di_San_Lanfranco.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/01/Martirio_di_Thomas_Becket_-_chiesa_di_San_Lanfranco.jpg/400px-Martirio_di_Thomas_Becket_-_chiesa_di_San_Lanfranco.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1116" data-file-height="732" /></a><figcaption>Fresco depicting the murder of Thomas Becket, on the left is the figure of Saint Lanfranco in act of blessing. <a href="/wiki/San_Lanfranco,_Pavia" title="San Lanfranco, Pavia">Church of San Lanfranco, Pavia</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>So a "major new feast day was instituted, commemorating the translation... celebrated each July almost everywhere in England and in many French churches."<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> It was suppressed in 1536 with the Reformation.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The shrine was destroyed in 1538 during the <a href="/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Monasteries" class="mw-redirect" title="Dissolution of the Monasteries">Dissolution of the Monasteries</a> on orders from King <a href="/wiki/Henry_VIII_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Henry VIII of England">Henry VIII</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-cch_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cch-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> He also destroyed Becket's bones and ordered all mention of his name obliterated.<sup id="cite_ref-cch_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cch-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As the scion of a mercantile dynasty of later centuries, <a href="/wiki/Mercers" class="mw-redirect" title="Mercers">Mercers</a>, Becket was much regarded as a Londoner by citizens and adopted as London's co-patron saint with <a href="/wiki/St_Paul" class="mw-redirect" title="St Paul">St Paul</a>: both appear on the seals of the city and of the Lord Mayor. The <a href="/wiki/Bridge_House_Estates" title="Bridge House Estates">Bridge House Estates</a> seal has only a Becket image, while his martyrdom shown on the reverse.
</p><p>The cult included the <a href="/wiki/Blood_as_food" title="Blood as food">drinking of "water of Saint Thomas"</a>, a mix of water and the remains of the martyr's blood miraculously multiplied. The procedure was frowned upon by the more orthodox, due to the similarities with the <a href="/wiki/Eucharist" title="Eucharist">eucharist</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Blood_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Blood of Jesus">blood of Jesus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Harvey_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harvey-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Local legends regarding Becket arose after his canonisation. Though they tend towards typical <a href="/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography">hagiography</a>, they also display Becket's well-known gruffness. "Becket's Well", in <a href="/wiki/Otford" title="Otford">Otford</a>, Kent, is said to have been created after Becket had been displeased by the taste of the local water. Two springs of clear water are said to have bubbled up after he struck the ground with his <a href="/wiki/Crozier" class="mw-redirect" title="Crozier">crozier</a>. The absence of nightingales in Otford is also ascribed to Becket, who is said to have been so disturbed in his devotions by the song of a nightingale that he commanded that none sing in the town ever again. In the town of <a href="/wiki/Strood" title="Strood">Strood</a>, Kent, Becket is said to have caused the inhabitants and their descendants to be born with tails. The men of Strood had sided with the king in his struggles against the archbishop, and to demonstrate their support had cut off the tail of Becket's horse as he passed through the town.
</p><p>The saint's fame quickly spread through the <a href="/wiki/Normans" title="Normans">Norman</a> world. The first holy image of Becket is thought to be a mosaic icon still visible in <a href="/wiki/Monreale_Cathedral" title="Monreale Cathedral">Monreale Cathedral</a> in <a href="/wiki/Sicily" title="Sicily">Sicily</a>, created shortly after his death. Becket's cousins obtained refuge at the Sicilian court during their exile, and King <a href="/wiki/William_II_of_Sicily" title="William II of Sicily">William II of Sicily</a> wed a daughter of Henry II. <a href="/wiki/Marsala_Cathedral" title="Marsala Cathedral">Marsala Cathedral</a> in western Sicily is dedicated to Becket. Over 45 medieval <a href="/wiki/Chasse_(casket)" title="Chasse (casket)">chasse</a> <a href="/wiki/Reliquaries" class="mw-redirect" title="Reliquaries">reliquaries</a> decorated in <a href="/wiki/Champlev%C3%A9_enamel" class="mw-redirect" title="Champlevé enamel">champlevé enamel</a> showing similar scenes from Becket's life survive, including the <a href="/wiki/Becket_Casket" title="Becket Casket">Becket Casket</a>, constructed to hold relics of him at <a href="/wiki/Peterborough_Cathedral" title="Peterborough Cathedral">Peterborough Abbey</a> and now housed in London's <a href="/wiki/Victoria_and_Albert_Museum" title="Victoria and Albert Museum">Victoria and Albert Museum</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Becket&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li>In 1170 King <a href="/wiki/Alfonso_VIII" class="mw-redirect" title="Alfonso VIII">Alfonso VIII</a> of Castille married <a href="/wiki/Eleanor_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Eleanor of England">Eleanor Plantagenet</a>, second daughter of Henry II. She honoured Becket with a wall painting of his martyrdom that survives in the church of <a href="/wiki/San_Nicol%C3%A1s_de_Soria" title="San Nicolás de Soria">San Nicolás de Soria</a> in Spain.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Becket's assassination made an impact in Spain: within five years of his death <a href="/wiki/Salamanca" title="Salamanca">Salamanca</a> had a church named after him, Iglesia de Santo Tomás Cantuariense. A monumental frescoes with the martyrdom of Thomas Becket were depicted in the romanesque church of Santa Maria at Terrassa.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_Chaucer" title="Geoffrey Chaucer">Geoffrey Chaucer</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/The_Canterbury_Tales" title="The Canterbury Tales">The Canterbury Tales</a></i> features a company of <a href="/wiki/Christian_pilgrimage" title="Christian pilgrimage">pilgrims</a> travelling from <a href="/wiki/Southwark" title="Southwark">Southwark</a> to Becket's shrine in Canterbury Cathedral.</li>
<li>The story of Becket's life became a popular theme for medieval <a href="/wiki/Nottingham_Alabaster" class="mw-redirect" title="Nottingham Alabaster">Nottingham Alabaster</a> carvers. One set of Becket panels is shown in the <a href="/wiki/Victoria_and_Albert_Museum" title="Victoria and Albert Museum">Victoria and Albert Museum</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The arms of the City of <a href="/wiki/Canterbury" title="Canterbury">Canterbury</a>, officially registered in 1619 but dating back to at least 1380, is based on the <a href="/wiki/Attributed_arms" title="Attributed arms">attributed arms</a> of Thomas Becket: <i>Argent, three Cornish choughs proper</i>, with the addition of a chief <i><a href="/wiki/Gules" title="Gules">gules</a></i> charged with <i>a lion passant guardant <a href="/wiki/Or_(heraldry)" title="Or (heraldry)">or</a></i> from the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Arms_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Royal Arms of England">Royal Arms of England</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In 1884, England's poet laureate <a href="/wiki/Alfred,_Lord_Tennyson" title="Alfred, Lord Tennyson">Alfred, Lord Tennyson</a> wrote <i><a href="/wiki/Becket_(Tennyson_play)" title="Becket (Tennyson play)">Becket</a></i>, a play about Thomas Becket and Henry II that <a href="/wiki/Henry_Irving" title="Henry Irving">Henry Irving</a> produced after Tennyson's death and played in the title role.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Modern works based on the Becket story include: <a href="/wiki/T._S._Eliot" title="T. S. Eliot">T. S. Eliot</a>'s play <i><a href="/wiki/Murder_in_the_Cathedral" title="Murder in the Cathedral">Murder in the Cathedral</a></i>, adapted as the opera <span title="Italian-language text"><i lang="it"><a href="/wiki/Assassinio_nella_cattedrale" title="Assassinio nella cattedrale">Assassinio nella cattedrale</a></i></span> by <a href="/wiki/Ildebrando_Pizzetti" title="Ildebrando Pizzetti">Ildebrando Pizzetti</a>); <a href="/wiki/Jean_Anouilh" title="Jean Anouilh">Jean Anouilh</a>'s play <i><a href="/wiki/Becket" title="Becket">Becket</a></i>, where Becket is not a Norman but a Saxon, adapted for the screen in 1964, and starring <a href="/wiki/Peter_O%27Toole" title="Peter O&#39;Toole">Peter O'Toole</a> and <a href="/wiki/Richard_Burton" title="Richard Burton">Richard Burton</a>; and Paul Webb's play <i><a href="/wiki/Four_Nights_in_Knaresborough" title="Four Nights in Knaresborough">Four Nights in Knaresborough</a></i>, which Webb adapted for the screen, selling the rights to <a href="/wiki/The_Weinstein_Company" title="The Weinstein Company">Harvey and Bob Weinstein</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> The power struggle between Church and King is a theme of <a href="/wiki/Ken_Follett" title="Ken Follett">Ken Follett</a>'s novel <i><a href="/wiki/The_Pillars_of_the_Earth" title="The Pillars of the Earth">The Pillars of the Earth</a></i>, where a late scene features the murder of Becket. Medieval mystery author <a href="/wiki/Jeri_Westerson" title="Jeri Westerson">Jeri Westerson</a> recreated Chaucer's <a href="/wiki/Christian_pilgrimage" title="Christian pilgrimage">pilgrims</a> and their time in Canterbury, with the murder and the theft of Becket's bones, in her fourth Crispin Guest novel, <i>Troubled Bones</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> An oratorio by David Reeves, <i>Becket (The Kiss of Peace)</i>, was premièred in 2000 at Canterbury Cathedral, where the event had occurred, as a part of the <a href="/wiki/Canterbury_Festival" title="Canterbury Festival">Canterbury Festival</a>, and a fundraiser for the <a href="/wiki/The_Prince%27s_Trust" title="The Prince&#39;s Trust">Prince's Trust</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Becket_Fund_for_Religious_Liberty" class="mw-redirect" title="Becket Fund for Religious Liberty">Becket Fund for Religious Liberty</a>, a non-profit, non-partisan legal and educational institute fostering free expression for religious traditions took its inspiration from Thomas Becket.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In a 2006 poll by <i><a href="/wiki/BBC_History" title="BBC History">BBC History</a></i> magazine for "worst Briton" of the previous millennium, Becket came second behind <a href="/wiki/Jack_the_Ripper" title="Jack the Ripper">Jack the Ripper</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-bbcworst_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bbcworst-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The poll was dismissed as "daft" in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Guardian" title="The Guardian">The Guardian</a></i>, and the result disputed by Anglicans and Catholics.<sup id="cite_ref-bbcworst_42-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bbcworst-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Historians had nominated one person per century, and for the 12th century <a href="/wiki/John_Hudson_(historian)" title="John Hudson (historian)">John Hudson</a> chose Becket for being "greedy", "hypocritical", "founder of gesture politics" and "master of the <a href="/wiki/Soundbite" class="mw-redirect" title="Soundbite">soundbite</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-bbcworst_42-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bbcworst-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> The magazine editor suggested most other nominees were too obscure for voters, as well as saying, "In an era when thumbscrews, racks and burning alive could be passed off as robust law and order—being guilty of 'gesture politics' might seem something of a minor charge."<sup id="cite_ref-bbcworst_42-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bbcworst-42">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The many UK churches dedicated to Thomas Becket include <a href="/wiki/Portsmouth_Cathedral" title="Portsmouth Cathedral">Cathedral Church of St Thomas of Canterbury, Portsmouth</a>, <a href="/wiki/St_Thomas_of_Canterbury_Church,_Canterbury" title="St Thomas of Canterbury Church, Canterbury">St Thomas of Canterbury Church, Canterbury</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Church_of_St_Thomas_the_Martyr,_Monmouth" title="Church of St Thomas the Martyr, Monmouth">Church of St Thomas the Martyr, Monmouth</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/St_Thomas_%C3%A0_Becket_Church,_Pensford" title="St Thomas à Becket Church, Pensford">St Thomas à Becket Church, Pensford</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/St_Thomas_%C3%A0_Becket_Church,_Widcombe" title="St Thomas à Becket Church, Widcombe">St Thomas à Becket Church, Widcombe</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Church_of_St_Thomas_%C3%A0_Becket,_Capel" title="Church of St Thomas à Becket, Capel">Church of St Thomas à Becket, Capel</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-cct_stb_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cct_stb-49">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/St_Thomas_the_Martyr,_Bristol" title="St Thomas the Martyr, Bristol">St Thomas the Martyr</a>, Bristol,<sup id="cite_ref-cct_stm_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cct_stm-50">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/St_Thomas_the_Martyr%27s_Church,_Oxford" title="St Thomas the Martyr&#39;s Church, Oxford">St Thomas the Martyr's Church, Oxford</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-near_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-near-51">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Those in France include Église Saint-Thomas de Cantorbéry at <a href="/wiki/Mont-Saint-Aignan" title="Mont-Saint-Aignan">Mont-Saint-Aignan</a>, Upper-Normandy,<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Église Saint-Thomas-Becket at <a href="/wiki/Gravelines" title="Gravelines">Gravelines</a> (Nord-Pas-de-Calais), Église Saint-Thomas Becket at <a href="/wiki/Avrieux" title="Avrieux">Avrieux</a> (Rhône-Alpes), and Église saint-Thomas Becket at <a href="/wiki/B%C3%A9nodet" title="Bénodet">Bénodet</a> (Brittany),<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Among his obligations in contrition to Henry, William de Tracy much enlarged and re-dedicated to St Thomas of Canterbury the parish church in <a href="/wiki/Lapford" title="Lapford">Lapford</a>, Devon, in his manor of <a href="/wiki/Bradninch" title="Bradninch">Bradninch</a>. The martyrdom day is still marked by a Lapford Revel.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2018)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></li>
<li>British schools named after Thomas Becket include <a href="/wiki/Becket_Keys_Church_of_England_School" title="Becket Keys Church of England School">Becket Keys Church of England School</a> and <a href="/wiki/St_Thomas_of_Canterbury_Church_of_England_Aided_Junior_School" title="St Thomas of Canterbury Church of England Aided Junior School">St Thomas of Canterbury Church of England Aided Junior School</a>.</li>
<li>Part of the Hungarian city of <a href="/wiki/Esztergom" title="Esztergom">Esztergom</a> is named <a href="/wiki/Szenttam%C3%A1s_(Esztergom)" title="Szenttamás (Esztergom)">Szenttamás</a> ("Saint Thomas"), on a hill called "Szent Tamás" dedicated to Thomas Becket – a classmate of <a href="/wiki/Lucas,_Archbishop_of_Esztergom" title="Lucas, Archbishop of Esztergom">Lucas, Archbishop of Esztergom</a> in Paris.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In the treasury of <a href="/wiki/Fermo_Cathedral" title="Fermo Cathedral">Fermo Cathedral</a> is <a href="/wiki/The_Fermo_chasuble_of_St._Thomas_Becket" class="mw-redirect" title="The Fermo chasuble of St. Thomas Becket">the Fermo chasuble of St. Thomas Becket</a>, on display at Museo Diocesano</li>
<li>Thomas Becket is honoured in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">Church of England</a> and in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church</a> on 29 December.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 235px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 230px; height: 230px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:SouthNewington_StPeterAdVincula_StThomasBeckettMartyrdom.JPG" class="mw-file-description" title="Wall painting of Thomas Becket&#39;s martyrdom painted in the 1330s in the parish church of St Peter ad Vincula, South Newington, Oxfordshire"><img alt="Wall painting of Thomas Becket&#39;s martyrdom painted in the 1330s in the parish church of St Peter ad Vincula, South Newington, Oxfordshire" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/SouthNewington_StPeterAdVincula_StThomasBeckettMartyrdom.JPG/200px-SouthNewington_StPeterAdVincula_StThomasBeckettMartyrdom.JPG" decoding="async" width="200" height="133" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/SouthNewington_StPeterAdVincula_StThomasBeckettMartyrdom.JPG/300px-SouthNewington_StPeterAdVincula_StThomasBeckettMartyrdom.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/SouthNewington_StPeterAdVincula_StThomasBeckettMartyrdom.JPG/400px-SouthNewington_StPeterAdVincula_StThomasBeckettMartyrdom.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2385" data-file-height="1590" /></a></span></div>

</p>
			</div>
		</li>
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 235px">
			<div class="thumb" style="width: 230px; height: 230px;"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Canterbury_Arms.svg" class="mw-file-description" title="The coat of arms of the City of Canterbury combines the attributed arms of Thomas Becket (three Cornish choughs) with a lion from the royal arms of England"><img alt="The coat of arms of the City of Canterbury combines the attributed arms of Thomas Becket (three Cornish choughs) with a lion from the royal arms of England" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Canterbury_Arms.svg/165px-Canterbury_Arms.svg.png" decoding="async" width="165" height="200" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Canterbury_Arms.svg/247px-Canterbury_Arms.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Canterbury_Arms.svg/330px-Canterbury_Arms.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="585" data-file-height="709" /></a></span></div>
			<div class="gallerytext">
<p>The coat of arms of the City of <a href="/wiki/Canterbury" title="Canterbury">Canterbury</a> combines the <a href="/wiki/Attributed_arms" title="Attributed arms">attributed arms</a> of Thomas Becket (three <a href="/wiki/Red-billed_chough" title="Red-billed chough">Cornish choughs</a>) with a lion from the <a href="/wiki/Royal_arms_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Royal arms of England">royal arms of England</a>
</p>
			</div>
		</li>
</ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="See_also">See also</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Becket&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: See also">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Portal:Catholic_Church/Patron_Archive/December_29" title="Portal:Catholic Church/Patron Archive/December 29">Saint Thomas Becket, patron saint archive</a></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Explanatory_notes">Explanatory notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Thomas_Becket&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Explanatory notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-3"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-3">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The name "Thomas à Becket" is not contemporary. It appears to be a post-<a href="/wiki/English_Reformation" title="English Reformation">Reformation</a> creation, possibly modelled on <a href="/wiki/Thomas_%C3%A0_Kempis" title="Thomas à Kempis">Thomas à Kempis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Barlow11_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Barlow11-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-8"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-8">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">There is a legend that claims Thomas's mother was a <a href="/wiki/Saracen" title="Saracen">Saracen</a> princess who met and fell in love with his English father while he was on Crusade or <a href="/wiki/Christian_pilgrimage" title="Christian pilgrimage">pilgrimage</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Land" title="Holy Land">Holy Land</a>, followed him home, was baptised and married him. This story has no truth to it, being a fabrication from three centuries after the saint's martyrdom, inserted as a forgery into <a href="/wiki/Edward_Grim" title="Edward Grim">Edward Grim</a>'s 12th-century <i>Life of St Thomas</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Matilda is occasionally known as Rohise.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_1-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
